<p><b>John Malcolm Fraser</b> <span style="font-size:85%;"><span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:100%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of Australia">AC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_the_Companions_of_Honour" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of the Companions of Honour">CH</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Grand_Companion_of_the_Order_of_Logohu" class="mw-redirect" title="Grand Companion of the Order of Logohu">GCL</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Her_Majesty%27s_Most_Honourable_Privy_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="Her Majesty&#39;s Most Honourable Privy Council">PC</a></span></span> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;f&#39; in &#39;find&#39;">f</span><span title="&#39;r&#39; in &#39;rye&#39;">r</span><span title="/eɪ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;face&#39;">eɪ</span><span title="&#39;z&#39; in &#39;zoom&#39;">z</span><span title="/ər/: &#39;er&#39; in &#39;letter&#39;">ər</span></span>/</a></span></span>; 21 May 1930 – 20 March 2015) was an Australian politician who served as the 22nd <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Australia">prime minister of Australia</a>, from 1975 to 1983, holding office as the leader of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party of Australia</a>.
</p><p>Fraser was raised on his father's <a href="/wiki/Sheep_station" title="Sheep station">sheep stations</a>, and after studying at <a href="/wiki/Magdalen_College,_Oxford" title="Magdalen College, Oxford">Magdalen College, Oxford</a>, returned to Australia to take over the family property in the <a href="/wiki/Western_District_(Victoria)" title="Western District (Victoria)">Western District</a> of <a href="/wiki/Victoria_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Victoria (Australia)">Victoria</a>. After an initial defeat <a href="/wiki/1954_Australian_federal_election" title="1954 Australian federal election">in 1954</a>, he was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">Australian House of Representatives</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1955_Australian_federal_election" title="1955 Australian federal election">1955 federal election</a>, as a <a href="/wiki/Member_of_parliament" title="Member of parliament">member of parliament</a> (MP) for the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Wannon" title="Division of Wannon">division of Wannon</a>. He was 25 at the time, making him one of the youngest people ever elected to parliament. When <a href="/wiki/Harold_Holt" title="Harold Holt">Harold Holt</a> became prime minister in 1966, Fraser was appointed <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_the_Army_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for the Army (Australia)">Minister for the Army</a>. After Holt's <a href="/wiki/Disappearance_of_Harold_Holt" title="Disappearance of Harold Holt">disappearance</a> and <a href="/wiki/1968_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="1968 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">replacement</a> by <a href="/wiki/John_Gorton" title="John Gorton">John Gorton</a>, Fraser became <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Education_(Australia)" title="Minister for Education (Australia)">Minister for Education and Science</a> (1968–1969) and then <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Defence_(Australia)" title="Minister for Defence (Australia)">Minister for Defence</a> (1969–1971). In 1971, Fraser resigned from cabinet and denounced Gorton as "unfit to hold the great office of prime minister"; this precipitated <a href="/wiki/1971_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" class="mw-redirect" title="1971 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">the replacement</a> of Gorton with <a href="/wiki/William_McMahon" title="William McMahon">William McMahon</a>. He subsequently returned to his old education and science portfolio.
</p><p>After the <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Liberal-National Coalition</a> was defeated at the <a href="/wiki/1972_Australian_federal_election" title="1972 Australian federal election">1972 election</a>, Fraser <a href="/wiki/1972_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="1972 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">unsuccessfully stood</a> for the Liberal leadership, losing to <a href="/wiki/Billy_Snedden" title="Billy Snedden">Billy Snedden</a>. When the party lost the <a href="/wiki/1974_Australian_federal_election" title="1974 Australian federal election">1974 election</a>, he began to move against Snedden, eventually mounting <a href="/wiki/1975_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="1975 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">a successful challenge</a> in March 1975. As <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Australia)" title="Leader of the Opposition (Australia)">Leader of the Opposition</a>, Fraser used the Coalition's control of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">Australian Senate</a> to block <a href="/wiki/Supply_(political_science)" class="mw-redirect" title="Supply (political science)">supply</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Whitlam_government" title="Whitlam government">Whitlam government</a>, precipitating the <a href="/wiki/1975_Australian_constitutional_crisis" title="1975 Australian constitutional crisis">1975 Australian constitutional crisis</a>. This culminated with <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Gough Whitlam</a> being dismissed as prime minister by the <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">governor-general</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Kerr_(governor-general)" title="John Kerr (governor-general)">Sir John Kerr</a>, a unique occurrence in Australian history. The correctness of Fraser's actions in the crisis and the exact nature of his involvement in Kerr's decision have since been a topic of debate. Fraser remains the only Australian prime minister to ascend to the position upon the dismissal of his predecessor.
</p><p>After Whitlam's dismissal, Fraser was sworn in as prime minister on an initial <a href="/wiki/Acting_(law)" title="Acting (law)">caretaker</a> basis. The Coalition won a <a href="/wiki/Landslide_victory" title="Landslide victory">landslide victory</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1975_Australian_federal_election" title="1975 Australian federal election">1975 election</a>, and was re-elected <a href="/wiki/1977_Australian_federal_election" title="1977 Australian federal election">in 1977</a> and <a href="/wiki/1980_Australian_federal_election" title="1980 Australian federal election">1980</a>. Fraser took a keen interest in foreign affairs as prime minister, and was more active in the international sphere than many of his predecessors. He was a strong supporter of <a href="/wiki/Multiculturalism_in_Australia" title="Multiculturalism in Australia">multiculturalism</a>, and during his term in office Australia admitted significant numbers of non-white immigrants (including <a href="/wiki/Vietnamese_boat_people" title="Vietnamese boat people">Vietnamese boat people</a>) for the first time, effectively ending the <a href="/wiki/White_Australia_policy" title="White Australia policy">White Australia policy</a>. His government also established the <a href="/wiki/Special_Broadcasting_Service" title="Special Broadcasting Service">Special Broadcasting Service</a> (SBS). Particularly in his final years in office, Fraser came into conflict with the "dry" <a href="/wiki/Economic_rationalism" title="Economic rationalism">economic rationalist</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fiscal_conservatism" title="Fiscal conservatism">fiscal conservative</a> faction of his party. His government made few major changes to economic policy.
</p><p>After losing the <a href="/wiki/1983_Australian_federal_election" title="1983 Australian federal election">1983 election</a>, Fraser retired from politics. In his post-political career, he held advisory positions with the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a> (UN) and the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth of Nations</a>, and was president of the aid agency <a href="/wiki/CARE_(relief_agency)" class="mw-redirect" title="CARE (relief agency)">CARE</a> from 1990 to 1995. He resigned his membership of the Liberal Party in 2009 after the election of <a href="/wiki/Tony_Abbott" title="Tony Abbott">Tony Abbott</a> as leader, Fraser having been a critic of the Liberals’ policy direction for a number of years. Evaluations of Fraser's prime ministership have been mixed. He is generally credited with restoring stability to the country after a series of short-term leaders and has been praised for his commitment to multiculturalism and opposition to <a href="/wiki/Apartheid" title="Apartheid">apartheid</a> in <a href="/wiki/South_Africa" title="South Africa">South Africa</a>, but the circumstances of his entry to office remains controversial and many have viewed his government as a lost opportunity for economic reform. His seven and a half-year tenure as prime minister is the fourth longest in Australian history, only surpassed by <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Howard" title="John Howard">John Howard</a> and <a href="/wiki/Robert_Menzies" title="Robert Menzies">Robert Menzies</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Birth_and_family_background">Birth and family background</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Birth and family background">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>John Malcolm Fraser was born in <a href="/wiki/Toorak,_Victoria" title="Toorak, Victoria">Toorak, Melbourne, Victoria</a>, on 21 May 1930. He was the second of two children born to Una Arnold (née Woolf) and <a href="/wiki/Neville_Fraser" title="Neville Fraser">John Neville Fraser</a>; his older sister Lorraine had been born in 1928. Both he and his father were known exclusively by their middle names. His paternal grandfather, <a href="/wiki/Simon_Fraser_(Australian_politician)" title="Simon Fraser (Australian politician)">Sir Simon Fraser</a>, was born in <a href="/wiki/Nova_Scotia" title="Nova Scotia">Nova Scotia</a>, Canada, and arrived in Australia in 1853. He made his fortune as a railway contractor, and later acquired significant <a href="/wiki/Pastoral_farming" title="Pastoral farming">pastoral</a> holdings, becoming a member of the "<a href="/wiki/Squattocracy" class="mw-redirect" title="Squattocracy">squattocracy</a>". Fraser's maternal grandfather, Louis Woolf, was born in <a href="/wiki/Dunedin" title="Dunedin">Dunedin</a>, New Zealand, and arrived in Australia as a child. He was of <a href="/wiki/Jewish" class="mw-redirect" title="Jewish">Jewish</a> origin, a fact which his grandson did not learn until he was an adult. A chartered accountant by trade, he married Amy Booth, who was related to the wealthy <a href="/wiki/Hordern_family" title="Hordern family">Hordern family</a> of Sydney and was a first cousin of <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Hordern" title="Samuel Hordern">Sir Samuel Hordern</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-simons_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-simons-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fraser had a political background on both sides of his family. His father served on the <a href="/wiki/Wakool_Shire" title="Wakool Shire">Wakool Shire</a> Council, including as president for two years, and was an admirer of <a href="/wiki/Billy_Hughes" title="Billy Hughes">Billy Hughes</a> and a friend of <a href="/wiki/Richard_Casey,_Baron_Casey" title="Richard Casey, Baron Casey">Richard Casey</a>. Simon Fraser served in both houses of the colonial <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Victoria" title="Parliament of Victoria">Parliament of Victoria</a>, and represented Victoria at several of the <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_Convention_(Australia)" title="Constitutional Convention (Australia)">constitutional conventions</a> of the 1890s. He eventually become one of the inaugural members of the new <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">federal Senate</a>, serving from 1901 to 1913 as a member of the early conservative parties. Louis Woolf also ran for the Senate in 1901, standing as a <a href="/wiki/Free_Trade_Party" title="Free Trade Party">Free Trader</a> in <a href="/wiki/Western_Australia" title="Western Australia">Western Australia</a>. He polled only 400 votes across the whole state, and was never again a candidate for public office.<sup id="cite_ref-simons_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-simons-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Childhood">Childhood</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Childhood">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Fraser spent most of his early life at <i>Balpool-Nyang</i>, a <a href="/wiki/Sheep_station" title="Sheep station">sheep station</a> of 15,000 hectares (37,000 acres) on the <a href="/wiki/Edward_River" title="Edward River">Edward River</a> near <a href="/wiki/Moulamein,_New_South_Wales" class="mw-redirect" title="Moulamein, New South Wales">Moulamein, New South Wales</a>. His father had a law degree from <a href="/wiki/Magdalen_College,_Oxford" title="Magdalen College, Oxford">Magdalen College, Oxford</a>, but never practised law and preferred the life of a grazier. Fraser contracted a severe case of pneumonia when he was eight years old, which nearly proved fatal. He was home-schooled until the age of ten, when he was sent to board at <a href="/wiki/Tudor_House_School" title="Tudor House School">Tudor House School</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Southern_Highlands_(New_South_Wales)" title="Southern Highlands (New South Wales)">Southern Highlands</a>. He attended Tudor House from 1940 to 1943, and then completed his secondary education at <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_Grammar_School" title="Melbourne Grammar School">Melbourne Grammar School</a> from 1944 to 1948 where he was a member of Rusden House. While at Melbourne Grammar, he lived in a flat that his parents owned on <a href="/wiki/Collins_Street,_Melbourne" title="Collins Street, Melbourne">Collins Street</a>. In 1943, Fraser's father sold <i>Balpool-Nyang</i> – which had been prone to drought – and bought <i>Nareen</i>, in the <a href="/wiki/Western_District_(Victoria)" title="Western District (Victoria)">Western District</a> of Victoria. He was devastated by the sale of his childhood home, and regarded the day he found out about it as the worst of his life.<sup id="cite_ref-simons_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-simons-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="University">University</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: University">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1949, Fraser moved to England to study at <a href="/wiki/Magdalen_College,_Oxford" title="Magdalen College, Oxford">Magdalen College, Oxford</a>, which his father had also attended. He read <a href="/wiki/Philosophy,_Politics_and_Economics" class="mw-redirect" title="Philosophy, Politics and Economics">Philosophy, Politics and Economics</a> (PPE), graduating in 1952 with <a href="/wiki/Third-class_honours" class="mw-redirect" title="Third-class honours">third-class honours</a>. Although Fraser did not excel academically, he regarded his time at Oxford as his intellectual awakening, where he learned "how to think". His college <a href="/wiki/Tutor_(education)" class="mw-redirect" title="Tutor (education)">tutor</a> was <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Dewar_Weldon" title="Thomas Dewar Weldon">Harry Weldon</a>, who was a strong influence. His circle of friends at Oxford included <a href="/wiki/Raymond_Bonham_Carter" title="Raymond Bonham Carter">Raymond Bonham Carter</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nicolas_Browne-Wilkinson,_Baron_Browne-Wilkinson" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicolas Browne-Wilkinson, Baron Browne-Wilkinson">Nicolas Browne-Wilkinson</a>, and <a href="/wiki/John_Turner" title="John Turner">John Turner</a>. In his second year, he had a relationship with Anne Reid, who as <a href="/wiki/Anne_Fairbairn" title="Anne Fairbairn">Anne Fairbairn</a> later became a prominent poet. After graduating, Fraser considered taking a law degree or joining the <a href="/wiki/British_Army" title="British Army">British Army</a>, but eventually decided to return to Australia and take over the running of the family property.<sup id="cite_ref-simons_1-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-simons-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career">Early political career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Early political career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Fraser returned to Australia in mid-1952. He began attending meetings of the <a href="/wiki/Young_Liberals_(Australia)" title="Young Liberals (Australia)">Young Liberals</a> in <a href="/wiki/Hamilton,_Victoria" title="Hamilton, Victoria">Hamilton</a>, and became acquainted with many of the local party officials. In November 1953, aged 23, Fraser unexpectedly won Liberal <a href="/wiki/Preselection" title="Preselection">preselection</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Wannon" title="Division of Wannon">Division of Wannon</a>, which covered most of Victoria's Western District. The previous Liberal member, <a href="/wiki/Dan_Mackinnon" title="Dan Mackinnon">Dan Mackinnon</a>, had been defeated in 1951 and moved to a different electorate. He was expected to be succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Magnus_Cormack" title="Magnus Cormack">Magnus Cormack</a>, who had recently lost his place in the Senate. Fraser had put his name forward as a way of building a profile for future candidacies, but mounted a strong campaign and in the end won a narrow victory.<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> In January 1954, he made the first of a series of weekly radio broadcasts on <a href="/wiki/3HA" class="mw-redirect" title="3HA">3HA</a> Hamilton and <a href="/wiki/3YB" class="mw-redirect" title="3YB">3YB</a> Warrnambool, titled <i>One Australia</i>. His program – consisting of a pre-recorded 15-minute monologue – covered a wide range of topics, and was often reprinted in newspapers. It continued more or less uninterrupted until his retirement from politics in 1983, and helped him build a substantial personal following in his electorate.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the <a href="/wiki/1954_Australian_federal_election" title="1954 Australian federal election">1954 election</a>, Fraser lost to the sitting <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Labor</a> member <a href="/wiki/Don_McLeod_(politician)" title="Don McLeod (politician)">Don McLeod</a> by just 17 votes (out of over 37,000 cast).<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> However, he reprised his candidacy at the early <a href="/wiki/1955_Australian_federal_election" title="1955 Australian federal election">1955 election</a> after a <a href="/wiki/Electoral_redistribution" class="mw-redirect" title="Electoral redistribution">redistribution</a> made Wannon notionally Liberal. McLeod concluded the reconfigured Wannon was unwinnable and retired. These factors, combined with the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_split_of_1955" title="Australian Labor Party split of 1955">1955 Labor Party split</a>, allowed Fraser to win a landslide victory.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Backbencher">Backbencher</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Backbencher">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fraser1956.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Fraser1956.jpg/170px-Fraser1956.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="223" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Fraser1956.jpg/255px-Fraser1956.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/95/Fraser1956.jpg 2x" data-file-width="324" data-file-height="425" /></a><figcaption>Fraser in 1956, shortly after his election to Parliament</figcaption></figure>
<p>Fraser took his seat in parliament at the age of 25 – the youngest sitting MP by four years, and the first who had been too young to serve in World War II.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He was re-elected at the <a href="/wiki/1958_Australian_federal_election" title="1958 Australian federal election">1958 election</a> despite being restricted in his campaigning by a bout of hepatitis.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Fraser was soon being touted as a future member of cabinet, but despite good relations with <a href="/wiki/Robert_Menzies" title="Robert Menzies">Robert Menzies</a> never served in cabinet during Menzies' tenure. His long wait for ministerial preferment was probably due to a combination of his youth and the fact that Menzies' ministries already contained a disproportionately high number of Victorians.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fraser spoke on a wide range of topics during his early years in parliament, but took a particular interest in foreign affairs. In 1964, he and Gough Whitlam were both awarded Leader Grants by the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Department_of_State" title="United States Department of State">United States Department of State</a>, allowing them to spend two months in Washington, D.C., getting to know American political and military leaders. The Vietnam War was the main topic of conversation, and on his return trip to Australia he spent two days in <a href="/wiki/Saigon" class="mw-redirect" title="Saigon">Saigon</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Early in 1965, he also made a private seven-day visit to <a href="/wiki/Jakarta" title="Jakarta">Jakarta</a>, and with assistance from Ambassador <a href="/wiki/Mick_Shann" title="Mick Shann">Mick Shann</a> secured meetings with various high-ranking officials.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cabinet_Minister_and_Gorton_downfall">Cabinet Minister and Gorton downfall</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Cabinet Minister and Gorton downfall">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:CSIRO_ScienceImage_2336_Visiting_the_Dish.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/CSIRO_ScienceImage_2336_Visiting_the_Dish.jpg/170px-CSIRO_ScienceImage_2336_Visiting_the_Dish.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="216" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/CSIRO_ScienceImage_2336_Visiting_the_Dish.jpg/255px-CSIRO_ScienceImage_2336_Visiting_the_Dish.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/CSIRO_ScienceImage_2336_Visiting_the_Dish.jpg/340px-CSIRO_ScienceImage_2336_Visiting_the_Dish.jpg 2x" data-file-width="744" data-file-height="945" /></a><figcaption>Fraser as Minister for Education and Science in 1969, with <a href="/wiki/John_England_(politician)" title="John England (politician)">John England</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1966, after more than a decade on the backbench, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Menzies" title="Robert Menzies">Sir Robert Menzies</a> retired as Prime Minister. His successor <a href="/wiki/Harold_Holt" title="Harold Holt">Harold Holt</a> appointed Fraser to the <a href="/wiki/First_Holt_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="First Holt Ministry">ministry</a> as <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Defence_(Australia)" title="Minister for Defence (Australia)">Minister for the Army</a>. In that position, Fraser presided over the controversial <a href="/wiki/Conscription_in_Australia#Vietnam_War" title="Conscription in Australia">Vietnam War conscription program</a>.
</p><p>Under the new prime minister, <a href="/wiki/John_Gorton" title="John Gorton">John Gorton</a>, he was elevated to <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Australia" title="Cabinet of Australia">Cabinet</a> as <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Education_(Australia)" title="Minister for Education (Australia)">Minister for Education and Science</a>. In 1969 he was promoted to <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Defence_(Australia)" title="Minister for Defence (Australia)">Minister for Defence</a>, a particularly challenging post at the time, given the height of Australia's involvement in the <a href="/wiki/Vietnam_War" title="Vietnam War">Vietnam War</a> and the protests against it.
</p><p>In March 1971 Fraser abruptly resigned from the Cabinet in protest at what he called Gorton's "interference in (his) ministerial responsibilities", and denounced Gorton on the floor of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">House of Representatives</a> as "not fit to hold the great office of Prime Minister".<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> This precipitated a series of events which eventually led to the downfall of Gorton and his replacement as prime minister by <a href="/wiki/William_McMahon" title="William McMahon">William McMahon</a>. In the <a href="/wiki/1971_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="1971 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">leadership contest</a> that followed Gorton's resignation, Fraser unsuccessfully contested the deputy Liberal leadership against Gorton and <a href="/wiki/David_Fairbairn_(politician)" title="David Fairbairn (politician)">David Fairbairn</a>. Gorton never forgave Fraser for the role he played in his downfall; to the day Gorton died in 2002, he could not bear to be in the same room with Fraser.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fraser remained on the backbenches until he was reinstated to Cabinet in his old position of Minister for Education and Science by McMahon in August 1971, immediately following Gorton's sacking as deputy Liberal leader by McMahon. When the Liberals were defeated at the <a href="/wiki/1972_Australian_federal_election" title="1972 Australian federal election">1972 election</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Labor Party</a> under <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Gough Whitlam</a>, McMahon resigned and Fraser became <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Employment_and_Workplace_Relations_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Employment and Workplace Relations (Australia)">Shadow Minister for Labour</a> under <a href="/wiki/Billy_Snedden" title="Billy Snedden">Billy Snedden</a>.
</p>
<h2><span id="Opposition_.281972.E2.80.931975.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition_(1972–1975)">Opposition (1972–1975)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Opposition (1972–1975)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>After the Coalition lost the <a href="/wiki/1972_Australian_federal_election" title="1972 Australian federal election">1972 election</a>, Fraser was <a href="/wiki/1972_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="1972 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">one of five candidates</a> for the Liberal leadership that had been vacated by McMahon. He outpolled John Gorton and <a href="/wiki/James_Killen" title="James Killen">James Killen</a>, but was eliminated on the third ballot. <a href="/wiki/Billy_Snedden" title="Billy Snedden">Billy Snedden</a> eventually defeated <a href="/wiki/Nigel_Bowen" title="Nigel Bowen">Nigel Bowen</a> by a single vote on the fifth ballot. In the new <a href="/wiki/Shadow_cabinet_of_Australia" title="Shadow cabinet of Australia">shadow cabinet</a> – which featured only Liberals – Fraser was given responsibility for primary industry. This was widely seen as a snub, as the new portfolio kept him mostly out of the public eye and was likely to be given to a member of the Country Party when the Coalition returned to government.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> In an August 1973 reshuffle, Snedden instead made him the Liberals' spokesman for industrial relations. He had hoped to be given responsibility for foreign affairs (in place of the retiring Nigel Bowen), but that role was given to <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Peacock" title="Andrew Peacock">Andrew Peacock</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-p213_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p213-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Fraser oversaw the development of the party's new industrial relations policy, which was released in April 1974. It was seen as more flexible and even-handed than the policy that the Coalition had pursued in government, and was received well by the media.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> According to Fraser's biographer <a href="/wiki/Philip_James_Ayres" title="Philip James Ayres">Philip Ayres</a>, by "putting a new policy in place, he managed to modify his public image and emerge as an excellent communicator across a traditionally hostile divide".<sup id="cite_ref-p213_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p213-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition">Leader of the Opposition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Leader of the Opposition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After the Liberals lost the <a href="/wiki/1974_Australian_federal_election" title="1974 Australian federal election">1974 election</a>, Fraser unsuccessfully challenged Snedden for the leadership in November. Despite surviving the challenge, Snedden's position in opinion polls continued to decline and he was unable to get the better of Whitlam in the Parliament. Fraser again challenged Snedden on 21 March 1975, this time succeeding and becoming Leader of the Liberal Party and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Australia)" title="Leader of the Opposition (Australia)">Leader of the Opposition</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Role_in_the_Dismissal">Role in the Dismissal</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Role in the Dismissal">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1975_Australian_constitutional_crisis" title="1975 Australian constitutional crisis">1975 Australian constitutional crisis</a></div>
<p>Following a series of ministerial scandals engulfing the <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Whitlam government</a> later that year, Fraser began to instruct Coalition senators to delay the government's budget bills, with the objective of forcing an early election that he believed he would win. After several months of political deadlock, during which time the government secretly explored methods of obtaining supply funding outside the Parliament, the <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">Governor-General</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Kerr_(Governor-General)" class="mw-redirect" title="John Kerr (Governor-General)">Sir John Kerr</a>, controversially dismissed Whitlam as prime minister on 11 November 1975.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fraser was immediately sworn in as <a href="/wiki/Acting_(law)" title="Acting (law)">caretaker</a> prime minister on the condition that he end the political deadlock and call an immediate <a href="/wiki/Double_dissolution" title="Double dissolution">double dissolution</a> election.
</p><p>On 19 November 1975, shortly after the election had been called, a letter bomb was sent to Fraser, but it was intercepted and defused before it reached him. Similar devices were sent to the governor-general and the <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_Queensland" title="Premier of Queensland">Premier of Queensland</a>, <a href="/wiki/Joh_Bjelke-Petersen" title="Joh Bjelke-Petersen">Joh Bjelke-Petersen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_.281975.E2.80.931983.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_(1975–1983)">Prime Minister (1975–1983)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Prime Minister (1975–1983)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Fraser_government" title="Fraser government">Fraser government</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1975_and_1977_federal_elections">1975 and 1977 federal elections</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: 1975 and 1977 federal elections">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fraser_Malcolm_BANNER.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Fraser_Malcolm_BANNER.jpg/170px-Fraser_Malcolm_BANNER.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="238" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Fraser_Malcolm_BANNER.jpg/255px-Fraser_Malcolm_BANNER.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/89/Fraser_Malcolm_BANNER.jpg 2x" data-file-width="257" data-file-height="360" /></a><figcaption>Fraser in 1976</figcaption></figure>
<p>At the <a href="/wiki/1975_Australian_federal_election" title="1975 Australian federal election">1975 election</a>, Fraser led the <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Liberal-Country Party Coalition</a> to a landslide victory. The Coalition won 91 seats of a possible 127 in the election to gain a 55-seat majority,<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> which remains to date the largest in Australian history. Fraser subsequently led the Coalition to a second victory in <a href="/wiki/1977_Australian_federal_election" title="1977 Australian federal election">1977</a>, with only a very small decrease in their vote. The Liberals actually won a majority in their own right in both of these elections, something that Menzies and Holt had never achieved. Although Fraser thus had no need for the support of the (National) Country Party to govern, he retained the formal Coalition between the two parties.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Fiscal_policy">Fiscal policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Fiscal policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fraser_-_Ford_-_Peck.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Fraser_-_Ford_-_Peck.jpg/220px-Fraser_-_Ford_-_Peck.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="145" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Fraser_-_Ford_-_Peck.jpg/330px-Fraser_-_Ford_-_Peck.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Fraser_-_Ford_-_Peck.jpg/440px-Fraser_-_Ford_-_Peck.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4555" data-file-height="3000" /></a><figcaption>Fraser at a <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a> state dinner in 1976, being introduced to actor <a href="/wiki/Gregory_Peck" title="Gregory Peck">Gregory Peck</a> by President <a href="/wiki/Gerald_Ford" title="Gerald Ford">Gerald Ford</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Fraser quickly dismantled some of the programs of the Whitlam government, such as the Ministry of the Media, and made major changes to the universal health insurance system <a href="/wiki/Medibank" title="Medibank">Medibank</a>. He initially maintained Whitlam's levels of tax and spending, but real per-person tax and spending soon began to increase. He did manage to rein in inflation, which had soared under Whitlam. His so-called "Razor Gang" implemented stringent budget cuts across many areas of the Commonwealth Public Sector, including the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Broadcasting_Corporation" title="Australian Broadcasting Corporation">Australian Broadcasting Corporation</a> (ABC).<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fraser practised <a href="/wiki/Keynesian" class="mw-redirect" title="Keynesian">Keynesian</a> economics during his time as Prime Minister,<sup id="cite_ref-naa_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-naa-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> in part demonstrated by running budget deficits throughout his term as Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-news_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-news-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> He was the Liberal Party's last Keynesian Prime Minister. Though he had long been identified with the Liberal Party's right wing, he did not carry out the radically conservative program that his political enemies had predicted, and that some of his followers wanted. Fraser's relatively moderate policies particularly disappointed the <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">Treasurer</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Howard" title="John Howard">John Howard</a>, as well as other ministers who were strong adherents of <a href="/wiki/Fiscal_conservatism" title="Fiscal conservatism">fiscal conservatism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Economic_liberalism" title="Economic liberalism">economic liberalism</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-naa_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-naa-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> and therefore detractors of Keynesian economics. The government's economic record was marred by rising double-digit unemployment and double-digit inflation, creating "<a href="/wiki/Stagflation" title="Stagflation">stagflation</a>", caused in part by the ongoing effects of the <a href="/wiki/1973_oil_crisis" title="1973 oil crisis">1973 oil crisis</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Foreign policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Fraser_Prime_Minister_of_Australia_and_Jimmy_Carter_during_a_state_visit_arrival_ceremony._-_NARA_-_175240.tif" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/John_Fraser_Prime_Minister_of_Australia_and_Jimmy_Carter_during_a_state_visit_arrival_ceremony._-_NARA_-_175240.tif/lossy-page1-170px-John_Fraser_Prime_Minister_of_Australia_and_Jimmy_Carter_during_a_state_visit_arrival_ceremony._-_NARA_-_175240.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="249" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/John_Fraser_Prime_Minister_of_Australia_and_Jimmy_Carter_during_a_state_visit_arrival_ceremony._-_NARA_-_175240.tif/lossy-page1-255px-John_Fraser_Prime_Minister_of_Australia_and_Jimmy_Carter_during_a_state_visit_arrival_ceremony._-_NARA_-_175240.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/John_Fraser_Prime_Minister_of_Australia_and_Jimmy_Carter_during_a_state_visit_arrival_ceremony._-_NARA_-_175240.tif/lossy-page1-340px-John_Fraser_Prime_Minister_of_Australia_and_Jimmy_Carter_during_a_state_visit_arrival_ceremony._-_NARA_-_175240.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2007" data-file-height="2941" /></a><figcaption>Fraser and US president <a href="/wiki/Jimmy_Carter" title="Jimmy Carter">Jimmy Carter</a> in June 1977</figcaption></figure>
<p>Fraser was particularly active in foreign policy as prime minister. He supported the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth</a> in campaigning to abolish <a href="/wiki/South_Africa_under_apartheid" class="mw-redirect" title="South Africa under apartheid">apartheid</a> in South Africa and refused permission for the aircraft carrying the <a href="/wiki/South_Africa_National_Rugby_Union_team" class="mw-redirect" title="South Africa National Rugby Union team">Springbok</a> rugby team to refuel on Australian territory en route to their controversial <a href="/wiki/1981_South_Africa_rugby_union_tour_of_New_Zealand" class="mw-redirect" title="1981 South Africa rugby union tour of New Zealand">1981 tour of New Zealand</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-SMH_cricket_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH_cricket-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> However, an earlier tour by the South African ski boat angling team was allowed to pass through Australia on the way to New Zealand in 1977 and the transit records were suppressed by Cabinet order.<sup id="cite_ref-Transit_suppressed_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Transit_suppressed-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fraser also strongly opposed white minority rule in <a href="/wiki/Rhodesia" title="Rhodesia">Rhodesia</a>. During the 1979 Commonwealth Conference, Fraser, together with his Nigerian counterpart, convinced the newly elected British prime minister, <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a>, to withhold recognition of the internal settlement <a href="/wiki/Zimbabwe_Rhodesia" title="Zimbabwe Rhodesia">Zimbabwe Rhodesia</a> government; Thatcher had earlier promised to recognise it. Subsequently, the <a href="/wiki/Lancaster_House_Agreement" title="Lancaster House Agreement">Lancaster House Agreement</a> was signed and <a href="/wiki/Robert_Mugabe" title="Robert Mugabe">Robert Mugabe</a> was elected leader of an independent <a href="/wiki/Zimbabwe" title="Zimbabwe">Zimbabwe</a> at the inaugural <a href="/wiki/1980_Zimbabwean_parliamentary_election" class="mw-redirect" title="1980 Zimbabwean parliamentary election">1980 election</a>. Duncan Campbell, a former deputy secretary of the Department of Foreign Affairs and Trade has stated that Fraser was "the principal architect" in the ending of white minority rule.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/President_of_Tanzania" title="President of Tanzania">President of Tanzania</a>, <a href="/wiki/Julius_Nyerere" title="Julius Nyerere">Julius Nyerere</a>, said that he considered Fraser's role "crucial in many parts" and the <a href="/wiki/President_of_Zambia" title="President of Zambia">President of Zambia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kenneth_Kaunda" title="Kenneth Kaunda">Kenneth Kaunda</a>, called his contribution "vital".<sup id="cite_ref-Colebatch_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Colebatch-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Under Fraser, Australia recognised <a href="/wiki/Indonesia" title="Indonesia">Indonesia</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Indonesian_occupation_of_East_Timor" title="Indonesian occupation of East Timor">annexation</a> of <a href="/wiki/East_Timor" title="East Timor">East Timor</a>, although many East Timorese refugees were granted <a href="/wiki/Asylum_in_Australia" title="Asylum in Australia">asylum in Australia</a>. 
</p><p>Fraser was also a strong supporter of the United States and supported the boycott of the <a href="/wiki/1980_Summer_Olympics" title="1980 Summer Olympics">1980 Summer Olympics</a> in Moscow. However, although he persuaded some sporting bodies not to compete, Fraser did not try to prevent the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Olympic_Committee" title="Australian Olympic Committee">Australian Olympic Committee</a> sending a team to the Moscow Games.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_policy">Other policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Other policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Fraser also surprised his critics over immigration policy; according to 1977 Cabinet documents, the Fraser government adopted a formal policy for "a humanitarian commitment to admit refugees for resettlement".<sup id="cite_ref-Australian_Snub_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Australian_Snub-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Fraser's aim was to expand immigration from Asian countries and allow more refugees to enter Australia. He was a firm supporter of <a href="/wiki/Multiculturalism" title="Multiculturalism">multiculturalism</a> and established a government-funded multilingual radio and television network, the <a href="/wiki/Special_Broadcasting_Service" title="Special Broadcasting Service">Special Broadcasting Service</a> (SBS), building on their first radio stations which had been established under the Whitlam government.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite Fraser's support for SBS, his government imposed stringent budget cuts on the national broadcaster, the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Broadcasting_Corporation" title="Australian Broadcasting Corporation">ABC</a>, which came under repeated attack from the Coalition for alleged "left-wing bias" and "unfair" coverage on their TV programs, including <i><a href="/wiki/This_Day_Tonight" title="This Day Tonight">This Day Tonight</a></i> and <i><a href="/wiki/Four_Corners_(Australian_TV_program)" title="Four Corners (Australian TV program)">Four Corners</a></i>, and on the ABC's new youth-oriented radio station <a href="/wiki/Triple_J" title="Triple J">Double Jay</a>. One result of the cuts was a plan to establish a national youth radio network, of which Double Jay was the first station. The network was delayed for many years and did not come to fruition until the 1990s. 
</p><p>Fraser also legislated to give <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_Australians" title="Indigenous Australians">Indigenous Australians</a> control of their traditional lands in the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Territory" title="Northern Territory">Northern Territory</a>, but resisted imposing land rights laws on conservative state governments.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1980_federal_election">1980 federal election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: 1980 federal election">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Ronald_Reagan_and_Nancy_Reagan_with_Prime_Minister_Malcolm_Fraser_and_Tamara_Fraser.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/President_Ronald_Reagan_and_Nancy_Reagan_with_Prime_Minister_Malcolm_Fraser_and_Tamara_Fraser.jpg/220px-President_Ronald_Reagan_and_Nancy_Reagan_with_Prime_Minister_Malcolm_Fraser_and_Tamara_Fraser.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="335" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/President_Ronald_Reagan_and_Nancy_Reagan_with_Prime_Minister_Malcolm_Fraser_and_Tamara_Fraser.jpg/330px-President_Ronald_Reagan_and_Nancy_Reagan_with_Prime_Minister_Malcolm_Fraser_and_Tamara_Fraser.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/President_Ronald_Reagan_and_Nancy_Reagan_with_Prime_Minister_Malcolm_Fraser_and_Tamara_Fraser.jpg/440px-President_Ronald_Reagan_and_Nancy_Reagan_with_Prime_Minister_Malcolm_Fraser_and_Tamara_Fraser.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2629" data-file-height="4000" /></a><figcaption>The Frasers with the <a href="/wiki/Ronald_Reagan" title="Ronald Reagan">Reagans</a> at the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a> in 1982</figcaption></figure>
<p>At the <a href="/wiki/1980_Australian_federal_election" title="1980 Australian federal election">1980 election</a>, Fraser saw his majority more than halved, from 48 seats to 21. The Coalition also lost control of the Senate. Despite this, Fraser remained ahead of Labor leader <a href="/wiki/Bill_Hayden" title="Bill Hayden">Bill Hayden</a> in opinion polls. However, the economy was hit by the <a href="/wiki/Early_1980s_recession" title="Early 1980s recession">early 1980s recession</a>, and a protracted scandal over tax-avoidance schemes run by some high-profile Liberals also began to hurt the government.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Disputes_within_the_Liberal_Party">Disputes within the Liberal Party</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Disputes within the Liberal Party">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In April 1981, the <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Employment_and_Workplace_Relations" title="Minister for Employment and Workplace Relations">Minister for Industrial Relations</a>, <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Peacock" title="Andrew Peacock">Andrew Peacock</a>, resigned from the Cabinet, accusing Fraser of "constant interference in his portfolio". Fraser, however, had accused former prime minister <a href="/wiki/John_Gorton" title="John Gorton">John Gorton</a> of the same thing a decade earlier. Peacock subsequently challenged Fraser for the leadership; although Fraser defeated Peacock, these events left him politically weakened.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Labor_Party_and_1983_federal_election">Labor Party and 1983 federal election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Labor Party and 1983 federal election">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:MalcolmFraser1982.JPEG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/MalcolmFraser1982.JPEG/170px-MalcolmFraser1982.JPEG" decoding="async" width="170" height="247" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/MalcolmFraser1982.JPEG/255px-MalcolmFraser1982.JPEG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/74/MalcolmFraser1982.JPEG 2x" data-file-width="327" data-file-height="475" /></a><figcaption>Fraser in 1982, towards the end of his tenure in office</figcaption></figure>
<p>By early 1982, the popular former <a href="/wiki/Australian_Council_of_Trade_Unions" title="Australian Council of Trade Unions">ACTU President</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a>, who had entered Parliament in 1980, was polling well ahead of both Fraser and the Labor Leader, <a href="/wiki/Bill_Hayden" title="Bill Hayden">Bill Hayden</a>, on the question of who voters would rather see as prime minister. Fraser was well aware of the infighting this caused between Hayden and Hawke and had planned to call a snap election in autumn 1982, preventing the Labor Party changing leaders. These plans were derailed when Fraser suffered a severe back injury. Shortly after recovering from his injury, the Liberal Party narrowly won a <a href="/wiki/1982_Flinders_by-election" title="1982 Flinders by-election">by-election</a> in the marginal seat of <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Flinders" title="Division of Flinders">Flinders</a> in December 1982. The failure of the Labor Party to win the seat convinced Fraser that he would be able to win an election against Hayden.
</p><p>As leadership tensions began to grow in the Labor Party throughout January, Fraser subsequently resolved to call a <a href="/wiki/Double_dissolution" title="Double dissolution">double dissolution</a> <a href="/wiki/1983_Australian_federal_election" title="1983 Australian federal election">election</a> at the earliest opportunity, hoping to capitalise on Labor's disunity. He knew that if the writs were issued soon enough, Labor would essentially be frozen into going into the subsequent election with Hayden as leader.
</p><p>On 3 February 1983, Fraser arranged to visit the <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">Governor-General of Australia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ninian_Stephen" title="Ninian Stephen">Sir Ninian Stephen</a>, intending to ask for a surprise election. However, Fraser made his run too late. Without any knowledge of Fraser's plans, Hayden resigned as Labor leader just two hours before Fraser travelled to <a href="/wiki/Government_House,_Canberra" title="Government House, Canberra">Government House</a>. This meant that the considerably more popular Hawke was able to replace him at almost exactly the same time that the writs were issued for the election. Although Fraser reacted to the move by saying he looked forward to "knock[ing] two Labor Leaders off in one go" at the forthcoming election, Labor immediately surged in the opinion polls.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the election on 5 March the Coalition was heavily defeated, suffering a 24-seat swing, the worst defeat of a non-Labor government since Federation. Fraser immediately announced his resignation as Liberal leader and formally resigned as prime minister on 11 March 1983; he retired from Parliament two months later. To date, he is the last non-interim prime minister from a rural seat.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement">Retirement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Retirement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In retirement Fraser was Chairman of the UN Panel of Eminent Persons on the Role of Transnational Corporations in South Africa 1985, as Co-Chairman of the Commonwealth Group of Eminent Persons on South Africa in 1985–86 (appointed by Prime Minister Hawke), and as Chairman of the UN Secretary-General's Expert Group on African Commodity Issues in 1989–90. He was a distinguished international fellow at the <a href="/wiki/American_Enterprise_Institute" title="American Enterprise Institute">American Enterprise Institute</a> from 1984 to 1986. Fraser helped to establish the foreign aid group <a href="/wiki/CARE_(relief_agency)" class="mw-redirect" title="CARE (relief agency)">CARE</a> organisation in Australia and became the agency's international president in 1991, and worked with a number of other charitable organisations.<sup id="cite_ref-Farquharson1_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Farquharson1-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> In 2006, he was appointed Professorial Fellow at the Asia Pacific Centre for Military Law, and in October 2007 he presented his inaugural professorial lecture, "Finding Security in Terrorism's Shadow: The importance of the rule of law".<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Memphis_trousers_affair">Memphis trousers affair</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Memphis trousers affair">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On 14 October 1986, Fraser, then the Chairman of the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Eminent_Persons_Group" title="Commonwealth Eminent Persons Group">Commonwealth Eminent Persons Group</a>, was found in the foyer of the Admiral Benbow Inn, a seedy <a href="/wiki/Memphis,_Tennessee" title="Memphis, Tennessee">Memphis</a> hotel, wearing only a pair of underpants and confused as to where his trousers were. The hotel was an establishment popular with prostitutes and drug dealers. Though it was rumoured at the time that the former Prime Minister had been with a prostitute, his wife stated that Fraser had no recollection of the events and that she believes it more likely that he was the victim of a practical joke by his fellow delegates.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Estrangement_from_the_Liberal_Party">Estrangement from the Liberal Party</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Estrangement from the Liberal Party">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:FraserSorry.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/FraserSorry.jpg/220px-FraserSorry.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/FraserSorry.jpg/330px-FraserSorry.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/FraserSorry.jpg/440px-FraserSorry.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="683" /></a><figcaption>Fraser at <a href="/wiki/Parliament_House,_Canberra" title="Parliament House, Canberra">Parliament House</a> in 2008, for <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Rudd" title="Kevin Rudd">Kevin Rudd</a>'s national apology to the <a href="/wiki/Stolen_Generations" title="Stolen Generations">Stolen Generations</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1993, Fraser made a bid for the Liberal Party presidency but withdrew at the last minute following opposition to his bid, which was raised due to his having been critical of then Liberal leader <a href="/wiki/John_Hewson" title="John Hewson">John Hewson</a> for losing the election earlier that year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESimons_&amp;_Fraser721_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESimons_&amp;_Fraser721-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After 1996, Fraser was critical of the <a href="/wiki/Howard_government" title="Howard government">Howard Coalition government</a> over foreign policy issues, particularly <a href="/wiki/John_Howard" title="John Howard">John Howard</a>'s alignment with the foreign policy of the <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">Bush</a> administration, which Fraser saw as damaging Australian relationships in Asia. He opposed Howard's policy on <a href="/wiki/Asylum-seekers" class="mw-redirect" title="Asylum-seekers">asylum-seekers</a>, campaigned in support of an <a href="/wiki/Republicanism_in_Australia" title="Republicanism in Australia">Australian Republic</a> and attacked what he perceived as a lack of integrity in Australian politics, together with former Labor prime minister <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Gough Whitlam</a>, finding much common ground with his predecessor and his successor <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a>, another republican.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/2001_Australian_federal_election" title="2001 Australian federal election">2001 election</a> continued his estrangement from the Liberal Party. Many Liberals criticised the Fraser years as "a decade of lost opportunity" on deregulation of the Australian economy and other issues. In early 2004, a <a href="/wiki/Young_Liberals_(Australia)" title="Young Liberals (Australia)">Young Liberal</a> convention in Hobart called for Fraser's life membership of the Liberal Party to be ended.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2006, Fraser criticised Howard Liberal government policies on areas such as refugees, terrorism and civil liberties, and that "if Australia continues to follow United States policies, it runs the risk of being embroiled in the <a href="/wiki/Conflict_in_Iraq" class="mw-redirect" title="Conflict in Iraq">conflict in Iraq</a> for decades, and a fear of <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a> in the Australian community will take years to eradicate". Fraser claimed that the way the Howard government handled the <a href="/wiki/David_Hicks" title="David Hicks">David Hicks</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cornelia_Rau" title="Cornelia Rau">Cornelia Rau</a> and <a href="/wiki/Vivian_Solon" title="Vivian Solon">Vivian Solon</a> cases was questionable.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 20 July 2007, Fraser sent an open letter to members of the large activist group <a href="/wiki/GetUp!" title="GetUp!">GetUp!</a>, encouraging members to support GetUp's campaign for a change in policy on Iraq including a clearly defined exit strategy.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Fraser stated: "One of the things we should say to the Americans, quite simply, is that if the United States is not prepared to involve itself in high-level diplomacy concerning Iraq and other Middle East questions, our forces will be withdrawn before Christmas."<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the defeat of the Howard government at the <a href="/wiki/2007_Australian_federal_election" title="2007 Australian federal election">2007 federal election</a>, Fraser claimed Howard approached him in a corridor, following a cabinet meeting in May 1977 regarding <a href="/wiki/Vietnamese_people" title="Vietnamese people">Vietnamese</a> <a href="/wiki/Refugee" title="Refugee">refugees</a>, and said: "We don't want too many of these people. We're doing this just for show, aren't we?" The claims were made by Fraser in an interview to mark the release of the 1977 cabinet papers. Howard, through a spokesman, denied having made the comment.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2007 Fraser gave a speech to Melbourne Law School on terrorism and "the importance of the rule of law,"<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> which Liberal MP <a href="/wiki/Sophie_Mirabella" title="Sophie Mirabella">Sophie Mirabella</a><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
condemned in January 2008, claiming errors and "either intellectual sloppiness or deliberate dishonesty", and claimed that he tacitly supported Islamic fundamentalism, that he should have no influence on foreign policy, and claimed his stance on the <a href="/wiki/War_on_terror" title="War on terror">war on terror</a> had left him open to caricature as a "frothing-at-the-mouth leftie".<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Shortly after <a href="/wiki/Tony_Abbott" title="Tony Abbott">Tony Abbott</a> won the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill,_2009" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill, 2009">2009 Liberal Party leadership spill</a>, Fraser ended his Liberal Party membership,<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> stating the party was "no longer a liberal party but a conservative party".<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_political_activity">Later political activity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Later political activity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Malcolm_Fraser_bust.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/Malcolm_Fraser_bust.jpg/170px-Malcolm_Fraser_bust.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="251" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/Malcolm_Fraser_bust.jpg/255px-Malcolm_Fraser_bust.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/Malcolm_Fraser_bust.jpg/340px-Malcolm_Fraser_bust.jpg 2x" data-file-width="989" data-file-height="1459" /></a><figcaption>Bust of Malcolm Fraser by political cartoonist, caricaturist and sculptor <a href="/wiki/Peter_Nicholson_(cartoonist)" title="Peter Nicholson (cartoonist)">Peter Nicholson</a> located in the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister%27s_Avenue" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime Minister&#39;s Avenue">Prime Minister's Avenue</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Ballarat_Botanical_Gardens" title="Ballarat Botanical Gardens">Ballarat Botanical Gardens</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In December 2011, Fraser was highly critical of the Australian government's decision (also supported by the Liberal Party Opposition) to permit the export of uranium to India, relaxing the Fraser government's policy of banning sales of uranium to countries that are not signatories of the <a href="/wiki/Nuclear_Non-Proliferation_Treaty" class="mw-redirect" title="Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty">Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2012, Fraser criticised the basing of US military forces in Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In late 2012, Fraser wrote a foreword for the journal <i>Jurisprudence</i> where he openly criticised the current state of human rights in Australia and the Western World. "It is a sobering thought that in recent times, freedoms hard won through centuries of struggle, in the United Kingdom and elsewhere have been whittled away. In Australia alone we have laws that allow the secret detention of the innocent. We have had a vast expansion of the power of intelligence agencies. In many cases the onus of proof has been reversed and the justice that once prevailed has been gravely diminished."<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In July 2013, Fraser endorsed <a href="/wiki/Australian_Greens" title="Australian Greens">Australian Greens</a> <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">Senator</a> <a href="/wiki/Sarah_Hanson-Young" title="Sarah Hanson-Young">Sarah Hanson-Young</a> for re-election in a television advertisement, stating she had been a "reasonable and fair-minded voice".<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fraser's books include <i>Malcolm Fraser: The Political Memoirs</i> (with <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Simons" title="Margaret Simons">Margaret Simons</a> – The Miegunyah Press, 2010) and  <i>Dangerous Allies</i> (Melbourne University Press, 2014), which warns of "strategic dependence" on the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> In the book and in talks promoting it, he criticised the concept of <a href="/wiki/American_exceptionalism" title="American exceptionalism">American exceptionalism</a> and <a href="/wiki/US_foreign_policy" class="mw-redirect" title="US foreign policy">US foreign policy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Marriage_and_children">Marriage and children</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Marriage and children">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Malcolm_and_Tamie_Fraser.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Malcolm_and_Tamie_Fraser.jpg/170px-Malcolm_and_Tamie_Fraser.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="180" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Malcolm_and_Tamie_Fraser.jpg/255px-Malcolm_and_Tamie_Fraser.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Malcolm_and_Tamie_Fraser.jpg/340px-Malcolm_and_Tamie_Fraser.jpg 2x" data-file-width="738" data-file-height="782" /></a><figcaption>Malcolm and Tamie Fraser at a political event in 1958</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 9 December 1956, Fraser married <a href="/wiki/Tamie_Fraser" title="Tamie Fraser">Tamara "Tamie" Beggs</a>, who was almost six years his junior. They had met at a New Year's Eve party, and bonded over similar personal backgrounds and political views. The couple had four children together: Mark (b. 1958), Angela (b. 1959), Hugh (b. 1963), and Phoebe (b. 1966). Tamie frequently assisted her husband in campaigning, and her gregariousness was seen as complementing his more shy and reserved nature. She advised him on most of the important decisions in his career, and in retirement he observed that "if she had been prime minister in 1983, we would have won".<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Views_on_religion">Views on religion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Views on religion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Fraser attended Anglican schools, although his parents were Presbyterian.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> In university he was inclined towards atheism, once writing that "the idea that God exists is a nonsense". However, his beliefs became less definite over time and tended towards agnosticism.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> During his political career, he occasionally self-described as Christian, such as in a 1975 interview with <i><a href="/wiki/The_Catholic_Weekly" title="The Catholic Weekly">The Catholic Weekly</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Simons" title="Margaret Simons">Margaret Simons</a>, the co-author of Fraser's memoirs, thought that he was "not religious, and yet thinks religion is a necessary thing". In a 2010 interview with her, he said: "I would probably like to be less logical and, you know, really able to believe there is a God, whether it is Allah, or the Christian God, or some other – but I think I studied too much philosophy ... you can never know".<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_legacy">Death and legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Death and legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Grave_of_John_Malcolm_Fraser,_Melbourne_General_Cemetery_2017.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/d/db/Grave_of_John_Malcolm_Fraser%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery_2017.jpg/220px-Grave_of_John_Malcolm_Fraser%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery_2017.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/d/db/Grave_of_John_Malcolm_Fraser%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery_2017.jpg/330px-Grave_of_John_Malcolm_Fraser%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery_2017.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/d/db/Grave_of_John_Malcolm_Fraser%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery_2017.jpg/440px-Grave_of_John_Malcolm_Fraser%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery_2017.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4032" data-file-height="3024" /></a><figcaption>Fraser's grave within the Prime Ministers Garden of <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_General_Cemetery" title="Melbourne General Cemetery">Melbourne General Cemetery</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Fraser died on 20 March 2015 at the age of 84, after a brief illness.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-abc-death_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abc-death-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> An <a href="/wiki/Obituary" title="Obituary">obituary</a> noted that there had been "greater appreciation of the constructive and positive nature of his post-prime ministerial contribution" as his retirement years progressed. Fraser's death came five months after that of his predecessor and political rival Gough Whitlam.<sup id="cite_ref-Farquharson1_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Farquharson1-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Upon his death, Fraser's 1983 nemesis and often bitter opponent <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a> fondly described him as a "very significant figure in the history of Australian politics" who, in his post-Prime Ministerial years, "became an outstanding figure in the advancement of human rights issues in all respects", praised him for being "extraordinarily generous and welcoming to refugees from Indochina" and concluded that Fraser had "moved so far to the left he was almost out of sight".<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Peacock" title="Andrew Peacock">Andrew Peacock</a>, who had <a href="/wiki/1982_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="1982 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">challenged</a> Fraser for the Liberal leadership and later succeeded him, said that he had "a deep respect and pleasurable memories of the first five years of the <a href="/wiki/Fraser_government" title="Fraser government">Fraser government</a>... I disagreed with him later on but during that period in the 1970s he was a very effective Prime Minister", and lamented that "despite all my arguments with him later on I am filled with admiration for his efforts on China".<sup id="cite_ref-ap_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ap-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fraser was given a state funeral at <a href="/wiki/Scots%27_Church,_Melbourne" title="Scots&#39; Church, Melbourne">Scots' Church</a> in Melbourne on 27 March 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> His ashes are interred within the Prime Ministers Garden of <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_General_Cemetery" title="Melbourne General Cemetery">Melbourne General Cemetery</a>.
</p><p>In 2004, Fraser designated the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Melbourne" title="University of Melbourne">University of Melbourne</a> the official custodian of his personal papers and library to create the Malcolm Fraser Collection at the university.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A street in <a href="/wiki/Abuja" title="Abuja">Abuja</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nigeria" title="Nigeria">Nigeria</a>, is named after Malcolm Fraser.
</p><p>In June 2018, he was honoured with the naming of the Australian Electoral <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Fraser_(Victoria)" title="Division of Fraser (Victoria)">Division of Fraser</a> in the inner north-western suburbs of Melbourne.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Published_works">Published works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Fraser&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Published works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
